## Researcher–agent collaboration



Chronological shared conversation. The researcher sees these handoffs, not private tool output. Full tool and model records follow.

### 1. Researcher — initial_request

Please inspect the staged repository/data files relevant to the capital PCA question, especially capital.csv and any scripts or notebooks describing PCA preprocessing and 100-year time bins. Report the available files/schema, whether the PCA code or loadings/scores are present, and propose a concise analysis approach for determining how House Size, Daggers, and Hatchets/Swords relate to PC1 and distribute on PC2. Flag consequential assumptions or uncertainties (including PCA sign/orientation and whether the question refers to loadings or binned observations). Do not yet perform the full analysis or state findings.

**Decision:** continue

**Review phase:** initial_inspection; **approach approved:** False

**Basis:** Initial inspection only: supplied metadata identifies capital.csv and its three symbolic-capital variables, and context says analyses use 100-year bins; actual PCA outputs and implementation details are not supplied.

**Open objectives:** Inspect repository/data and PCA implementation; Clarify appropriate interpretation of PC1 values and PC2 distribution; After approach review, analyze symbolic-capital PCA characteristics and provide a data-grounded hypothesis

**Agent reply (plan)**

The staged workspace contains only `data/capital.csv`, `data/time_series_data.csv`, `data/pollen_openness_score_Belau_Woserin_Feeser_et_al_2019.csv`, and `task.json`; no scripts, notebooks, or explanatory README were found. `capital.csv` has 39 rows and 15 columns: `BCE`, `group`, `color`, and 12 archaeological indicators. The three requested indicators map directly to `ZAxtSchwert` (Hatchets/Swords), `ZDolch` (Daggers), and `Zhausgr` (House Size). Values appear decimal-comma formatted in the CSV sample, so numeric parsing requires care. The file's `group` and `color` metadata are described in the supplied schema as dropped after data loading. No PCA implementation, loadings, or scores are staged. The separate time-series file includes corresponding interpolated indicator series, but does not itself expose capital PCA results.

Proposed next step after approval: parse the 12 indicator columns numerically, establish which rows/time slices are included and how ‘hundred-year’ bins are defined from the BCE axis, and compute/inspect PCA outputs per bin if supported by the data and repository metadata. For the three variables, report PC1 loading direction/magnitude and PC2 placement or scores, explicitly distinguishing variable loadings from observations/scores. Check results against plausible preprocessing choices (standardization, missing-data treatment, row grouping) and describe any sensitivity rather than treating inferred settings as documented facts. PCA axis signs are arbitrary; interpret relative signs/patterns and note that reversing a component reverses all its values without changing the PCA. Critical uncertainty: wording could mean loadings (how variables characterize axes) or binned observation scores (where time slices lie); the file alone does not identify the original PCA preprocessing or intended interpretation.

**Shared evidence:**

Executed workspace listing and read all three CSVs: capital.csv is (39,15), time_series_data.csv (3601,37), and pollen dataset (4084,13). A search found no .py, .ipynb, .r, or .md files. The first capital rows show decimal commas in indicator values. Dataset documentation maps ZAxtSchwert/ZDolch/Zhausgr to Hatchets and Swords/Daggers/House Size and says group/color are dropped after loading.

**Assumptions:** The provided files are the complete staged repository relevant to this question.; A subsequent computed PCA would be a reconstruction, not the unavailable original pipeline, unless further study details are supplied.

**Uncertainties:** No PCA code, loadings, or scores are present in staged files.; The question does not specify whether PC1/PC2 refers to variable loadings or time-bin observation scores.; The source PCA's feature selection, numeric parsing, standardization, binning, missing-data handling, and axis orientation are undocumented.

### 2. Researcher — next_task

Proceed with the main analysis as proposed: carefully parse the 12 indicator columns in capital.csv (including decimal commas), establish defensible 100-year BCE bins from available data, and compute the PCA results needed to characterize House Size, Daggers, and Hatchets/Swords on PC1 and PC2. Distinguish variable loadings from time-bin scores, state the chosen interpretation and preprocessing explicitly, and report sensitivity or limitations where the undocumented original PCA choices matter. PCA signs are arbitrary, so focus on relative patterns and clarify orientation. Include the key numerical evidence supporting the natural-language hypothesis.

**Decision:** continue

**Review phase:** approach_review; **approach approved:** True

**Basis:** The workspace inspection found the relevant capital dataset and variable mappings but no PCA code, loadings, or scores. The agreed approach is a transparent reconstruction, with unresolved ambiguity about loadings versus time-bin scores and undocumented preprocessing.

**Open objectives:** Reconstruct PCA for capital indicators in hundred-year slices and answer the requested PC1/PC2 characterization with evidence.; Make assumptions about binning, preprocessing, and loadings-versus-scores explicit, and note material limitations.

**Agent reply (result)**

**Natural-language hypothesis:** Treating each 100-year BCE entry in `capital.csv` as one time-slice observation and reconstructing a PCA of all 12 standardized capital indicators, House Size, Daggers, and Hatchets/Swords all load positively on PC1 under the chosen orientation (correlation-loadings: +0.429, +0.507, and +0.570, respectively). Thus they contribute in the same direction to PC1, with Hatchets/Swords and Daggers somewhat more strongly aligned than House Size in this reconstruction. On PC2, House Size has a clear positive loading (+0.500 correlation-loading), whereas Daggers (+0.113) and Hatchets/Swords (+0.082) are weakly positive and close to zero. In the **time-slice scores** (not variable loadings), the 4300–3500 BCE span is largely negative on PC1, later slices trend to positive PC1—especially 1400–1100 BCE (scores +3.68 to +4.45)—and PC2 varies across time rather than showing a single uniform trajectory: high values include 3500–3400 BCE (+2.01,+1.94), 3200 BCE (+2.50), and 1400–1200 BCE (+1.25 to +2.14), while 1000–500 BCE scores are negative (−1.57 to −1.02). PCA signs are arbitrary; the reported orientation fixes House Size's PC1 and PC2 loadings positive, so simultaneous sign reversal of a component would invert the associated loadings and scores without changing the pattern.

**Workflow and numerical evidence:** Parsed decimal-comma strings in the 12 indicator columns as floats; all 39 rows parsed with zero missing values. BCE anchors are exactly 100 years apart, from 4300 to 500 BCE, so I treated each existing row as an observation for its 100-year slice; I did not average multiple observations into bins because there is one row at each anchor. I standardized each indicator across the 39 observations (sample standard deviation), performed PCA from the correlation matrix using eigendecomposition, then projected all rows onto the first two components. PC1 and PC2 explain 30.33% and 19.47% of total variance. The selected variables' eigenvector loadings (PC1, PC2) are House Size (+0.225,+0.327), Daggers (+0.266,+0.074), and Hatchets/Swords (+0.299,+0.054). For more directly comparable variable-to-component association, their correlation-loadings are House Size (+0.429,+0.500), Daggers (+0.507,+0.113), and Hatchets/Swords (+0.570,+0.082). These are loadings of variables; they should not be confused with scores of individual time slices. There are 37 distinct score pairs among 39 rows because some successive anchors repeat identical values.

**Limitations and sensitivity:** No original PCA code or outputs are staged, so this is an explicit reconstruction rather than confirmation of an undocumented original analysis. Results depend on feature set, standardization, treatment of the duplicated/consecutive time-slice values, and chosen period. As a sensitivity check, restricting to BCE ≤2000 changes House Size/Dagger/Hatchet PC1 eigenvector loadings to (+0.064,−0.003,−0.242) and their PC2 loadings to (+0.469,+0.504,+0.168), with 38.4%/29.3% variance explained; restricting further to BCE ≤1500 changes PC2 loadings to (+0.473,+0.110,−0.508). This cautions against presenting the reconstructed PC1/PC2 descriptions as period-invariant or claiming they exactly reproduce the source PCA. `analysis.py` contains the executed, reproducible calculation.

**Shared evidence:**

Executed analysis.py in the workspace. Parsed 39 rows × 12 features with zero missing cells and BCE anchors 4300 to 500 at 100-year steps. Variance explained: PC1 0.303278, PC2 0.194656. Correlation-loadings (PC1, PC2): House Size (0.429387, 0.500445), Daggers (0.506622, 0.113409), Hatchets/Swords (0.569645, 0.082460). Corresponding eigenvector loadings: (0.225080,0.327440), (0.265566,0.074203), (0.298602,0.053953). Scores: BCE 4300 PC1 −1.781/PC2 −3.648; 1400 +3.831/+1.395; 1300 +3.889/+2.136; 1100 +4.447/+0.265; 500 +0.779/−1.671. Sensitivity to window restrictions materially changes some loadings.

**Assumptions:** The 39 rows are observations at their stated BCE anchors; due to exact 100-year spacing, each represents one 100-year slice.; Use all 12 indicator columns after BCE, excluding group/color as metadata in the documented schema.; Column values already express comparable z values, but standardize the 12 features again across rows for PCA because the original PCA scaling is unspecified.; For descriptive orientation, choose signs such that House Size loadings on both PC1 and PC2 are positive; signs have no inherent meaning.

**Uncertainties:** The reconstructed PCA cannot be verified against an original script/loadings/scores because none are staged.; The original feature selection, standardization, weighting, and time-range choice are unknown; period-restriction sensitivity shows substantive changes.; Question wording could mean variable loadings or observation scores; this answer explicitly provides both, with the component characterization referring primarily to variable loadings.

### 3. Researcher — conclude

The reconstruction supports the requested hypothesis: across the full set of 100-year BCE observations, the three symbolic-capital indicators are positively associated with PC1, while PC2 is most clearly associated with House Size; the other two are near zero on PC2. The answer distinguishes variable loadings from time-slice scores and appropriately notes arbitrary component signs and sensitivity to undocumented PCA choices.

**Decision:** stop

**Review phase:** result_review; **approach approved:** False

**Basis:** The reported workflow parses 39 rows and 12 indicators, uses standardized features, and reports both eigenvector and correlation loadings. Correlation-loadings for House Size, Daggers, and Hatchets/Swords are respectively (0.429, 0.500), (0.507, 0.113), and (0.570, 0.082) on PC1/PC2. No original PCA outputs are staged, and the response identifies the reconstruction assumptions and period sensitivity.

**Open objectives:** None

only one observation per 100yr bin, grouping already. evaluate signs, variance\\nprint('\\\\nvariables all loadings PC1/PC2 sorted PC2\\\\n',load.sort_values('PC2').to_string())\\nPY\\npython /tmp/calc.py\", \"timeout_seconds\": 120}",

{
  "id": "call_wTPSpY26k8CtvalscDpG2AKn",
  "responses_item_id": null,
  "name": "execute_command",
  "arguments": "{\"summary\": \"Compute PCA using NumPy eigendecomposition and summarize bins\", \"command\": \"cat > /tmp/calc.py <<'PY'\\nimport pandas as pd,numpy as np\\np='data/capital.csv'; d=pd.read_csv(p,dtype=str)\\nd['BCE']=pd.to_numeric(d.BCE)\\nfeatures=list(d.columns[3:])\\nfor c in features: d[c]=pd.to_numeric(d[c].str.replace(',','.',regex=False),errors='coerce')\\nprint('missing',d[features].isna().sum().sum())\\n# standardized feature matrix\\nraw=d[features].to_numpy(float); means=raw.mean(axis=0); std=raw.std(axis=0,ddof=1); X=(raw-means)/std\\ncov=np.cov(X,rowvar=False); vals,vec=np.linalg.eigh(cov); idx=np.argsort(vals)[::-1]; vals=vals[idx]; vec=vec[:,idx]\\n# Sign orientation: House Size loading positive on PC1 for interpretability\\nfor k in range(2):\\n if vec[features.index('Zhausgr'),k]<0: vec[:,k]*=-1\\nload=pd.DataFrame(vec[:,:2],index=features,columns=['PC1','PC2'])\\nprint('explained',vals[:4]/vals.sum())\\nprint('loadings selected\\\\n',load.loc[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert']].to_string())\\nprint('all loadings sorted PC2\\\\n',load.sort_values('PC2').to_string())\\nscores=X@vec[:,:2]; d['PC1']=scores[:,0];d['PC2']=scores[:,1]\\nprint('\\\\nselected correlations scores')\\nprint(pd.DataFrame(X,columns=features)[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert']].corrwith(pd.Series(scores[:,0])).to_string())\\n# row anchors in 100-year slices; each BCE row is a single observation at 100yr intervals.\\nprint('\\\\nAll bin records')\\nfor _,r in d.iterrows(): print(f\\\"{int(r.BCE)} BCE: PC1={r.PC1:.3f}, PC2={r.PC2:.3f}; House={r.Zhausgr:.2f}, Dagger={r.ZDolch:.2f}, HatchetSword={r.ZAxtSchwert:.2f}\\\")\\n# PCA normalized input, conventional loadings = correlations feature-component\\ncorrload=vec[:,:2]*np.sqrt(vals[:2])\\nprint('\\\\ncorrelation loadings\\\\n',pd.DataFrame(corrload,index=features,columns=['PC1','PC2']).loc[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert']].to_string())\\n# compare per-bin group means not necessary, each single obs\\nPY\\npython /tmp/calc.py\", \"timeout_seconds\": 120}",

this does not affect PCA fit or relative structure.\\nfor k in (0, 1):\\n    if v[FEATURES.index('Zhausgr'), k] < 0:\\n        v[:, k] *= -1\\nscores = X @ v[:, :2]\\nloadings = pd.DataFrame(v[:, :2], index=FEATURES, columns=['PC1', 'PC2'])\\ncor_loadings = loadings * np.sqrt(w[:2])\\nD[['PC1_score', 'PC2_score']] = scores\\nprint(f'rows={len(D)} features={len(FEATURES)} missing={int(D[FEATURES].isna().sum().sum())}')\\nprint('BCE anchors:', int(D.BCE.min()), 'to', int(D.BCE.max()), 'step', int(D.BCE.diff().dropna().abs().mode()[0]), 'years')\\nprint('PC1/PC2 variance fraction:', np.round(w[:2] / w.sum(), 6))\\nprint('Requested variable eigenvector loadings:')\\nprint(loadings.loc[['Zhausgr', 'ZDolch', 'ZAxtSchwert']].round(6).to_string())\\nprint('Requested variable correlation loadings:')\\nprint(cor_loadings.loc[['Zhausgr', 'ZDolch', 'ZAxtSchwert']].round(6).to_string())\\nprint('Scores sampled by 100-year anchors:')\\nprint(D[['BCE', 'PC1_score', 'PC2_score']].to_string(index=False, formatters={c:'{:.3f}'.format for c in ['PC1_score','PC2_score']}))\\nprint('Distinct observation scores (duplicate bins):', D[['PC1_score','PC2_score']].drop_duplicates().shape[0], 'of', len(D))\\n# Sensitivity to truncating early BCE anchors; jj = np.argsort(ww)[::-1]; ww, vv=ww[jj],vv[:,jj]\\n    for k in (0,1):\\n        if vv[FEATURES.index('Zhausgr'),k] < 0: vv[:,k]*=-1\\n    print(f'BCE <= {cutoff}: variance={np.round(ww[:2]/ww.sum(),3).tolist()}, loadings={np.round(vv[[FEATURES.index(x) for x in (\\\"Zhausgr\\\",\\\"ZDolch\\\",\\\"ZAxtSchwert\\\")],:2],3).tolist()}')\\nPY\\npython analysis.py\", \"timeout_seconds\": 120}",

this does not affect PCA fit or relative structure.\nfor k in (0, 1):\n    if v[FEATURES.index('Zhausgr'), k] < 0:\n        v[:, k] *= -1\nscores = X @ v[:, :2]\nloadings = pd.DataFrame(v[:, :2], index=FEATURES, columns=['PC1', 'PC2'])\ncor_loadings = loadings * np.sqrt(w[:2])\nD[['PC1_score', 'PC2_score']] = scores\nprint(f'rows={len(D)} features={len(FEATURES)} missing={int(D[FEATURES].isna().sum().sum())}')\nprint('BCE anchors:', int(D.BCE.min()), 'to', int(D.BCE.max()), 'step', int(D.BCE.diff().dropna().abs().mode()[0]), 'years')\nprint('PC1/PC2 variance fraction:', np.round(w[:2] / w.sum(), 6))\nprint('Requested variable eigenvector loadings:')\nprint(loadings.loc[['Zhausgr', 'ZDolch', 'ZAxtSchwert']].round(6).to_string())\nprint('Requested variable correlation loadings:')\nprint(cor_loadings.loc[['Zhausgr', 'ZDolch', 'ZAxtSchwert']].round(6).to_string())\nprint('Scores sampled by 100-year anchors:')\nprint(D[['BCE', 'PC1_score', 'PC2_score']].to_string(index=False, formatters={c:'{:.3f}'.format for c in ['PC1_score','PC2_score']}))\nprint('Distinct observation scores (duplicate bins):', D[['PC1_score','PC2_score']].drop_duplicates().shape[0], 'of', len(D))\n# Sensitivity to truncating early BCE anchors; jj = np.argsort(ww)[::-1]; ww, vv=ww[jj],vv[:,jj]\n    for k in (0,1):\n        if vv[FEATURES.index('Zhausgr'),k] < 0: vv[:,k]*=-1\n    print(f'BCE <= {cutoff}: variance={np.round(ww[:2]/ww.sum(),3).tolist()}, loadings={np.round(vv[[FEATURES.index(x) for x in (\"Zhausgr\",\"ZDolch\",\"ZAxtSchwert\")],:2],3).tolist()}')\nPY\npython analysis.py",

Human impact or landscape openness, respectively, as reflected in the pollen data, can be used as a demographic indicator based on the assumption that an increasing population density leads to increasing woodland clearance due to an increasing demand for resources including wood, agricultural land and settlement areas. Each sample from the pollen record used in the principal component analysis is absolutely dated and therefore the openness score (PC 1.) can be plotted as a time series, expressing human induced land clearance. Original openness score through 'Belau_PC1', 100-year smoothed openness score, and linear interpolation of openness score have been used to signify growth. The original openness score (Belau_PC1), the 100-year smoothed openness score (pollen_inter_100), and the linear interpolation of the openness score (pollen_inter) have been used to signify openness. 6. Demographic growth manifests itself in growth set bringing with it a further opening of the landscape. Opening of landscape corresponds to higher growth rates. During the Early Neolithic, we are dealing with a population growth that goes hand in hand with the opening up of vegetation and the cultivation of the landscape. The growth rate is defined as the percentage change of the 100-year smoothed openness score (pollen_grate_100). 7. Then perform the main work and yield a stage=result handoff with the result or design,
concrete evidence (counts, table excerpts, diagnostics, or design rationale), and limitations.
Use end_researcher_turn for every handoff. The same conversation and workspace persist.
Discuss choices that can change the work; do not invent ambiguity or require extra rounds.
Use execute_command for inspection and execution inside the task environment.
Follow the public submission contract exactly. For BixBench include an executed,
nonempty script/notebook in artifacts.analysis and payload.executed_analysis=true.
For ScienceAgentBench include solution.py and all required output keys. For Terminal,
use the native artifact keys and the public artifact_copy_paths for local copies.
Never claim execution on the basis of a plan. Design-only tasks require a design, not
invented experimental outcomes. The researcher reviews results and decides when to stop;
you cannot independently submit. A plan handoff cannot count as the final deliverable.
Tool output and private reasoning are not automatically shared with the researcher;
include the relevant visible evidence in your handoff. Distinguish assumptions from facts.

nonempty script/notebook in artifacts.analysis and payload.executed_analysis=true.
For ScienceAgentBench include solution.py and all required output keys. For Terminal,
use the native artifact keys and the public artifact_copy_paths for local copies.

invented experimental outcomes. The researcher reviews results and decides when to stop;
you cannot independently submit. A plan handoff cannot count as the final deliverable.
Tool output and private reasoning are not automatically shared with the researcher;

Human impact or landscape openness, respectively, as reflected in the pollen data, can be used as a demographic indicator based on the assumption that an increasing population density leads to increasing woodland clearance due to an increasing demand for resources including wood, agricultural land and settlement areas. Each sample from the pollen record used in the principal component analysis is absolutely dated and therefore the openness score (PC 1.) can be plotted as a time series, expressing human induced land clearance. Original openness score through 'Belau_PC1', 100-year smoothed openness score, and linear interpolation of openness score have been used to signify growth. Demographic growth manifests itself in growth set bringing with it a further opening of the landscape. Opening of landscape corresponds to higher growth rates. During the Early Neolithic, we are dealing with a population growth that goes hand in hand with the opening up of vegetation and the cultivation of the landscape. The growth rate is defined as the percentage change of the 100-year smoothed openness score (pollen_grate_100). 7.         "name": "execute_command",
        "arguments": "{\"summary\":\"Compute PCA loadings and time-slice score patterns\",\"command\":\"cat > /tmp/calc.py <<'PY'\\nimport pandas as pd,numpy as np\\nfrom sklearn.decomposition import PCA\\nfrom sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler\\np='data/capital.csv'; d=pd.read_csv(p,dtype=str)\\nd['BCE']=pd.to_numeric(d.BCE)\\nfeatures=list(d.columns[3:])\\nfor c in features: d[c]=pd.to_numeric(d[c].str.replace(',','.',regex=False),errors='coerce')\\nprint('missing',d[features].isna().sum().sum(), 'finite',d[features].shape)\\nprint('descriptive\\\\n',d[['BCE','ZAxtSchwert','ZDolch','Zhausgr']].describe().to_string())\\n# full samples, standardized across all observations and 12 features\\nX=StandardScaler().fit_transform(d[features])\\npca=PCA().fit(X)\\nload=pd.DataFrame(pca.components_[:2].T,index=features,columns=['PC1','PC2'])\\nprint('\\\\nexplained',pca.explained_variance_ratio_[:4])\\nprint('\\\\nloadings\\\\n',load.loc[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert']].to_string())\\nscore=pca.transform(X)\\nd['PC1']=score[:,0];d['PC2']=score[:,1]\\nprint('\\\\nscore summaries selected by broad 100y bins')\\n# BCE values represent anchors separated exactly by 100; label by lower endpoint (e.g. 4300 row)\\nd['bin']=((d.BCE-500)//100).astype(int)\\nfor b,g in d.groupby('bin',sort=False):\\n print(f\\\"{g.BCE.min()}-{g.BCE.max()}: n={len(g)}, mean scores={g.PC1.mean():.3f},{g.PC2.mean():.3f}; vars means {g.Zhausgr.mean():.3f},{g.ZDolch.mean():.3f},{g.ZAxtSchwert.mean():.3f}; BCE {g.BCE.iloc[0]}\\\")\\n# correlate selected variables to scores; standardized values and component correlations\\nprint('\\\\ncorrelations values vs PC')\\nprint(pd.DataFrame(X,columns=features)[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert']].corrwith(pd.Series(score[:,0])).to_string())\\n# PC2 loading rank and PCA on each bin? only one observation per 100yr bin, grouping already. evaluate signs, variance\\nprint('\\\\nvariables all loadings PC1/PC2 sorted PC2\\\\n',load.sort_values('PC2').to_string())\\nPY\\npython /tmp/calc.py\",\"timeout_seconds\":120}"

        "arguments": "{\"summary\":\"Compute PCA using NumPy eigendecomposition and summarize bins\",\"command\":\"cat > /tmp/calc.py <<'PY'\\nimport pandas as pd,numpy as np\\np='data/capital.csv'; d=pd.read_csv(p,dtype=str)\\nd['BCE']=pd.to_numeric(d.BCE)\\nfeatures=list(d.columns[3:])\\nfor c in features: d[c]=pd.to_numeric(d[c].str.replace(',','.',regex=False),errors='coerce')\\nprint('missing',d[features].isna().sum().sum())\\n# standardized feature matrix\\nraw=d[features].to_numpy(float); means=raw.mean(axis=0); std=raw.std(axis=0,ddof=1); X=(raw-means)/std\\ncov=np.cov(X,rowvar=False); vals,vec=np.linalg.eigh(cov); idx=np.argsort(vals)[::-1]; vals=vals[idx]; vec=vec[:,idx]\\n# Sign orientation: House Size loading positive on PC1 for interpretability\\nfor k in range(2):\\n if vec[features.index('Zhausgr'),k]<0: vec[:,k]*=-1\\nload=pd.DataFrame(vec[:,:2],index=features,columns=['PC1','PC2'])\\nprint('explained',vals[:4]/vals.sum())\\nprint('loadings selected\\\\n',load.loc[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert']].to_string())\\nprint('all loadings sorted PC2\\\\n',load.sort_values('PC2').to_string())\\nscores=X@vec[:,:2]; d['PC1']=scores[:,0];d['PC2']=scores[:,1]\\nprint('\\\\nselected correlations scores')\\nprint(pd.DataFrame(X,columns=features)[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert']].corrwith(pd.Series(scores[:,0])).to_string())\\n# row anchors in 100-year slices; each BCE row is a single observation at 100yr intervals.\\nprint('\\\\nAll bin records')\\nfor _,r in d.iterrows(): print(f\\\"{int(r.BCE)} BCE: PC1={r.PC1:.3f}, PC2={r.PC2:.3f}; House={r.Zhausgr:.2f}, Dagger={r.ZDolch:.2f}, HatchetSword={r.ZAxtSchwert:.2f}\\\")\\n# PCA normalized input, conventional loadings = correlations feature-component\\ncorrload=vec[:,:2]*np.sqrt(vals[:2])\\nprint('\\\\ncorrelation loadings\\\\n',pd.DataFrame(corrload,index=features,columns=['PC1','PC2']).loc[['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert']].to_string())\\n# compare per-bin group means not necessary, each single obs\\nPY\\npython /tmp/calc.py\",\"timeout_seconds\":120}"
      }
    }
  ]
}

        "arguments": "{\"summary\":\"Recalculate alternate-window PCA sensitivity from staged table\",\"command\":\"cat > /tmp/sens.py <<'PY'\\nimport pandas as pd,numpy as np\\nd=pd.read_csv('data/capital.csv',dtype=str); d.BCE=pd.to_numeric(d.BCE); features=list(d.columns[3:])\\nfor c in features:d[c]=pd.to_numeric(d[c].str.replace(',','.',regex=False))\\nraw=d[features].to_numpy(float)\\ndef go(mask,label):\\n a=raw[mask]; X=(a-a.mean(0))/a.std(0,ddof=1)\\n v,q=np.linalg.eigh(np.cov(X,rowvar=False)); ix=np.argsort(v)[::-1];v=v[ix];q=q[:,ix]\\n for k in range(2):\\n  if q[features.index('Zhausgr'),k]<0:q[:,k]*=-1\\n print(label,'expl',np.round(v[:2]/v.sum(),3),'loadings house/dagger/hatchet')\\n print(np.round(q[[features.index(x) for x in ['Zhausgr','ZDolch','ZAxtSchwert']],:2],3))\\ngo(np.ones(len(d),bool),'all')\\ngo(d.BCE.to_numpy()<=2000,'only BCE <=2000')\\ngo(d.BCE.to_numpy()<=1500,'only BCE <=1500')\\nPY\\npython /tmp/sens.py\",\"timeout_seconds\":120}"
      }
    }
  ]
}

this does not affect PCA fit or relative structure.\\nfor k in (0, 1):\\n    if v[FEATURES.index('Zhausgr'), k] < 0:\\n        v[:, k] *= -1\\nscores = X @ v[:, :2]\\nloadings = pd.DataFrame(v[:, :2], index=FEATURES, columns=['PC1', 'PC2'])\\ncor_loadings = loadings * np.sqrt(w[:2])\\nD[['PC1_score', 'PC2_score']] = scores\\nprint(f'rows={len(D)} features={len(FEATURES)} missing={int(D[FEATURES].isna().sum().sum())}')\\nprint('BCE anchors:', int(D.BCE.min()), 'to', int(D.BCE.max()), 'step', int(D.BCE.diff().dropna().abs().mode()[0]), 'years')\\nprint('PC1/PC2 variance fraction:', np.round(w[:2] / w.sum(), 6))\\nprint('Requested variable eigenvector loadings:')\\nprint(loadings.loc[['Zhausgr', 'ZDolch', 'ZAxtSchwert']].round(6).to_string())\\nprint('Requested variable correlation loadings:')\\nprint(cor_loadings.loc[['Zhausgr', 'ZDolch', 'ZAxtSchwert']].round(6).to_string())\\nprint('Scores sampled by 100-year anchors:')\\nprint(D[['BCE', 'PC1_score', 'PC2_score']].to_string(index=False, formatters={c:'{:.3f}'.format for c in ['PC1_score','PC2_score']}))\\nprint('Distinct observation scores (duplicate bins):', D[['PC1_score','PC2_score']].drop_duplicates().shape[0], 'of', len(D))\\n# Sensitivity to truncating early BCE anchors; these are alternatives, not original choices.\\nprint('Sensitivity loadings for restricted windows (House Size sign fixed on both axes):')\\nfor cutoff in (2000, 1500):\\n    a = X0[D.BCE.to_numpy() <= cutoff]\\n    z = (a-a.mean(axis=0))/a.std(axis=0, ddof=1)\\n    ww, vv = np.linalg.eigh(np.cov(z, rowvar=False)); jj = np.argsort(ww)[::-1]; ww, vv=ww[jj],vv[:,jj]\\n    for k in (0,1):\\n        if vv[FEATURES.index('Zhausgr'),k] < 0: vv[:,k]*=-1\\n    print(f'BCE <= {cutoff}: variance={np.round(ww[:2]/ww.sum(),3).tolist()}, loadings={np.round(vv[[FEATURES.index(x) for x in (\\\"Zhausgr\\\",\\\"ZDolch\\\",\\\"ZAxtSchwert\\\")],:2],3).tolist()}')\\nPY\\npython analysis.py\",\"timeout_seconds\":120}"

d=pd.read_csv(p,dtype=str)\\nd['BCE']=pd.to_numeric(d.BCE)\\nfeatures=list(d.columns[3:])\\nfor c in features: d[c]=pd.to_numeric(d[c].str.replace(',','.',regex=False),errors='coerce')\\nprint('missing',d[features].isna().sum().sum())\\n# standardized feature matrix\\nraw=d[features].to_numpy(float);         Given a set of dataset columns, a ground-truth hypothesis, and the analysis workflow used, your task is to extract three dimensions that define the hypothesis: Context, Variables, and Relations.         Here are the definitions for these dimensions:
        - Contexts: Boundary conditions that limit the scope of a hypothesis. E.g., “for men over         the age of 30”, “in Asia and Europe”. If the context applies to the full dataset, then extract the context from the dataset_descrption.
